[Abstract] At present, future medical imaging is increasingly being used to diagnose patients with cancer or other life-threatening problems without a physician's input (1⇓⇓–4). In fact, this level of patient monitoring has emerged as an urgent need and a cost effective alternative to a conventional surgical strategy of operationally treating disease when clinical findings warrant it (5, 6). However, current techniques are relatively costly compared to human surgeons who still perform a significant portion of total imaging-related revenue (7). Consequently, healthcare systems must now prioritize not just what images should be captured but how they ought to be collected and organized (8). To date, many experts have agreed that large volumes do require to be recorded (9) for certain types of health issues such as cardiac infarction detection in which information is relevant beyond simply allowing doctors to detect atrial fibrillation [6]. These guidelines also dictate use of standardized storage methods for storing medically useful clinical information on large-scale data centers (10), although others would like to see greater transparency about which institutions hold the most medical data, thereby enabling citizens to decide whether privacy is important when making healthcare decisions using mass data (11). Despite these differences, no consensus approach exists regarding the best way forward with respect to both capturing mass imaging images and maintaining appropriate access, quality and security while recording them on centralized data sources. This raises the question: Should we seek solutions which maximize efficiency while minimizing the amount stored or rely only on technologies

We introduce edge flow, a new class of directed graph structures capable of operating as interactive segmentation procedures on large distributed networks. We evaluate its performance by training two approaches to classify images in real time using two different ways of computing the error functions over image pixels (correlation and covariance). These methods jointly characterize an image's boundaries automatically, yielding reliable boundary detection in practical data sets, such as Facebook photos, videos, and Web pages. In addition, we demonstrate that these methods can produce accurate model invariants across images at low cost and are very sensitive to small inputs; thus they provide a fast path towards scalable algorithms for performing real-time segmentation operations over larger datasets. Examining supervisory learning methods and their application to medical images allows us to understand the current state of the art, identify potential areas where improvements should be made, and provide insight into the problems that need to addressed in future work. We analyze supervised contrastive labeling (SCL) techniques, which classify images by color alone or color & monochrome subgroups based on both categorical attributes as well as semantic categories of image features like brightness or contrast using only categorical features (e.g., luminance/contrast). The SCL algorithm we develop here relies on two steps: (1) preprocessing the inputs into labels, (2) separating out the training targets from the test data using the classifier output labeled with class labels. It then identifies specific target boundaries from unlabeled input space as indicated by edge separation coefficients. Specifically, the distance between an edge and its neighbors must deviate significantly less than or equal to 1 when applying the classification method; otherwise, the classifier outputs an error indicating that the output classification was inaccurate. A multi-organ identification (MOSI) algorithm was developed to handle input (input layer, i.e., nonclass specific masks or unigroups), output (output layer with output labels), and combination of the two (combination LUT). The objective function is computed using an integrated form that takes into account both training and testing data (training data can be either fixed or dynamic) when there are no significant differences between MOSI scores for any class labels. The resulting model uses a maximum likelihood estimation technique based on Monte Carlo bootstrapping to compute the posterior distributions for input/output match pairs. By calculating optimal score weights after learning several combinations of MISO parameters in three separate training samples, this algorithm achieves over 95% sensitivity against noise. This work demonstrates how multi-organ recognition systems such as those described by our collaborators have been applied successfully.

Abstract 3D seismic tomography data were acquired on land, offshore and at sea to provide a detailed 2D view of fault structures and earthquake epicenters in this region. With a few labels, we trained and evaluated two methods (the method 1 in Equations (2–3)) of segmentating faults into different segments, one for each label, with respect to their position near or away from the surface. In experiments conducted under various noise assumptions, it was possible to classify large earthquakes onshore as either major, medium-, low- or unknown events. A classification accuracy of 90%+ of earthquake events was achieved after 10+ trial runs over 5 weeks using an 80% overlap with existing models. The method 1 approach outperformed all other techniques except the random clustering results obtained by adding up the labeled images and dividing them by their lengthwise distance. Our evaluations show that many of our training labels do well in identifying strong earthquakes and that future work can focus more attention on developing label-based searcoder models when generating high quality 3D seismic tomographic maps for seismic hazard mitigation purposes. —Miguel C. Garcia

Abstract Non-automated image segmentation for facial reference identification is extremely challenging even with large data sets, especially when there are multiple eyes and/or nose pointing to a given face in an array representation. In this work we present such a scalable automatic system for automated multi-modality image segmentations based both on the similarity between two images taken at high resolution and their relative orientation similarity at all pixel resolutions (e.g., depth). The resulting classification results are compared at various reference types for human or robot recognition tasks: facial, occlusion and hair matching. Compared to previous state-of-the-art approaches, our approach uses more sparse feature representations from higher resolutions while maintaining good performance for different training images at the same spatial resolution (i.e., ~400×500). Our algorithm has excellent localization accuracy with very low error rate under similar test conditions using a variety of object attributes including depth, face shape parameters, orientation and texture data. We demonstrate our method's robustness by applying it to five widely used object data formats that make up a wide range of real world applications, yielding successful model learning for facial match, occlusion detection and partial occlusion classifying as well as hair type matches. Furthermore, the overall improvement over previously published methods on several categories is considerable. Overall, these results suggest that our methodology is effective enough during general task requirements and that it could be further developed through tuning its framework for faster iteration times. Additionally, our method may also have application in robotics where human-robot interactions

We present a novel method to automatically segment scenes composed of millions of pixels from large data sets by extracting coarse and fine gradients from their noise images. The method achieves good results on both visual objects, where features are robust against noisy data, and semantic or object representation problems involving small groups (e.g., a facial expression). (2011), while maintaining low computational demand [16]. The basic idea that underlies our algorithm is similar to that introduced by Vignolo et al. (2014), although it also adopts the notion of perceptual continuity in contrast to their perceptually discontinuous method. In general terms, we propose that visual input consists of pixels. These are categorized into different types with respect to their role in perceiving a scene [17], namely, lines, colors, depth information, edges, etc.. The field of image segmentation in neuroscience has been developing rapidly in recent years, and there are many challenges that can be addressed. We consider here a problem where these challenges collide and where they create the greatest value; namely, the use in medical imaging to infer what part of the brain has been damaged or destroyed by disease. In this work, we present an approach based on direct cortical and subcortical surface morphometry. During this work, we focus entirely in two directions: (1) We use cortical and subcortical surface morphology as indicators of structural change associated with disease and (2) the development of a robust method which allows reconstruction of complex images directly from cortical data, allowing us to recover a highly structured representation of large numbers of voxels without requiring a knowledge of any other structures or their properties. Our central hypothesis is that once defined well enough, this representation will be capable to reconstruct almost perfectly realistic faces from simple images.

Background: Chase is a system that supports real-time, visual tracking through spatial gradient information. Chasing uses neural networks to learn predictive models of path directions and trajectories given the location of each cell in a 3D scene. A recent set of papers provides evidence for recurrent network architectures which can provide an adequate framework for both deep learning and supervised and unsupervised hierarchical clustering tasks; however these hierarchies are poorly understood (see [22], [23], [25] ). In this work we address one critical issue which has prevented previous approaches: When given a training dataset composed solely of target cells at known position coordinates, why does the neural net converge well on a consistent representation? We propose to solve this problem using dynamic coding with low temporal resolution and by identifying differentially active neurons along each branch of the tree. The resulting model enables visual localization capabilities as large or larger than those used in traditional linear image sequence classification. Our implementation achieves high accuracy within 30 seconds during tests on simple indoor images (e.g., outdoor images). There is no doubt that we are in dire need of better ways of handling the problem where deep learning and regression problems do not fit our requirements, which are highly complicated segmentation task tasks such as feature extraction or object recognition. The current data processing models implemented in standard distributed algorithms are not suited well enough for these problems. A good example for simple and parallel application was shown by Gao et al., who applied convolutional networks and DNNs respectively on a 2D dataset. We present here an alternative solution by introducing the new concept of inter-extremity geoderricks (IER): a set of georeferenced geodesic line representations. These are not strictly convex line representations but they approximate this condition for a subset of points along a curve whose edge passes through no part of a point (i.e. their segment). Erecting a set that fits all points as per feature extraction and object detection schemes (based on local minima) can reduce the number and distance search distance by about 10 times, thereby dramatically reducing computation time and memory requirement (and improving accuracy), due largely to better preprocessing step. Our work further focuses on modeling inter-point constraints to determine the location of a set of subgroups defined at many scales, i.e. all parts or edge points. Introduction Our aim with this work was to propose a compact convolutional neural network in order to achieve high quality (f0/√n>9), and large scale use, where image segmentations may be performed as part of classification or semantic tasks and not as a side effect of training other deep learning models. Currently, image segmentations are typically handled by convnets that perform millions of backpropagation steps in order to classify complex images into many dimensions and then find an appropriate way to compute pixel estimates and corresponding feature maps. This approach involves extensive computation of tensor product spaces, several tensor normalizations within each dimension space, a complete data preprocessing step, the detection of outliers, the calculation of an effective minimum mean squared error distance norm, interpolators, the computation of sparse derivatives of distances between pixels using sparse derivatives, cross-correlated gradients, multiple batching, the generation of small samples along the pipeline via dropout, batch normalization across different data streams, etc. Each of these tasks has its own overhead associated with it - i.e., they require a lot of computationally expensive operations. We tried to solve a similar problem but with a much improved data processing stage with our CombiNet framework which will handle all of these preprocessing stages but also can calculate small sample distributions automatically. The most significant drawback with current approaches is their low accuracy rates given these heavy operations. However, many challenges still exist with supervised learning: Unveiling hidden structure that may not be revealed by data-correlation techniques (e.g., classification); overfitting; nonlocal interactions leading to excessive nonlinearities or other unanticipated consequences; etc. These problems are more difficult when trained using network architecture not yet fully adapted. The ability to extract hierarchical representations from unmodeled features of complex scenes could provide a way out of this current state because it would let network architectures learn very specific representations by themselves without using examples. We present a new algorithm called TransformNet which incorporates convolutions into its architecture and is based on a bimodal biphasic neuron network model for neural networks that we obtained through joint work with Ting Zhang at University College London. In contrast to simple convolutions, our biphasic convolution produces a biquadratic kernel at each node and the resulting kernels encode three dimensions: depth first, then width and then height. Our aim was to produce a deep convolution layer (conv1d) whose output features were dimensionally similar to those found in any three dimensional image dataset (image2vec), despite being only five layers deep. The use of multidimensional local distribution (MDL) methods results in more information being extracted. We compare our experiments on a large dataset of 1000 images and show that we can reduce reconstruction time by two orders of magnitude compared to state of art methods, such as SIFT or SVM classification. Furthermore, many MDL approaches have been proposed for different problem domains. For example, our implementation performs significantly better than those of Gaussians as described in previous work and supports multiple classification tasks including regression classification and machine translation. We present a model, based on neural networks with latent Dirichlet allocation (LD) architecture, that enables one to generate a deep convolutional network (CNN) for processing an extensive visual input and extracting features from it in real time, by exploiting latent information hidden within image structures such as edges between neighboring regions. The CNN consists of four layer with three hidden units per node along with two output activations shared among all four layers. We demonstrate significant superiority over state-of-the-art classification methods using 2D thresholding and linear regression to achieve good results across image classes ranging from faces, cat faces, medical images or road signs to street scenes, car driving lines or city landscape architecture. Further, our evaluation leads us to conclude that our approach has superior performance even over classical convolutional nets when trained on large scale data sets without prior supervised learning. Our results emphasize the importance of capturing more deeply hidden feature vectors which was not done until now during conventional segmentation algorithms, and show how segmentation problems can also be easily solved on real-time streaming signals generated using distributed computation. Connection structure provides the basic insight that images are composed of small regions—nodes, edges or vertices—that communicate and interact with one another in meaningful ways across a large volume of semantic space. This notion has been well-tethered to the idea of word embeddings on an existing neural network context; however, it appears that these same conceptual constructs may also be used by a new approach to connectionist verification without any explicit mapping to word embeddings. RLCcorrector adds reinforcement learning functionality to this traditional proofreading paradigm (see [1] http://arxiv.org/abs/1308.4824), and integrates it with existing machine pattern recognition algorithms such as Gaussian Process and Markov chains. RLCcorrectors allow for both error detection (using unsupervised feature learning) and inference (by using high-level supervised models that utilize the features present in the evidence). For errors, we use word vectors that encode information about the locations of the locations of words within a sentence and then detect similar occurrences in different documents. With the current algorithm, if two adjacent documents have not yet touched each other, our model suggests that their occurrence should correspond to some common location. A few moments later when they begin crossing over, the error becomes more apparent. Thus, this system can quickly learn patterns such as the positions of words contained inside sentences based on prior knowledge to correct erroneous matches that might otherwise elude prediction.[2][3] We show how RLCcorrectors can extend commonly used automatic annotation systems like Wordnet and Snippets to include connectionist approaches, adding validation and support information.

Abstract This paper investigates two fundamental questions, namely (i) how accurately one can make reliable, exact reconstructions of graphs (in graph theory) given their degree-4 sum-of-squares hierarchy (which is a particular representation scheme but may be well approximated by some other such schemes not discussed here), and (ii) how it generally works at different types of computational problems - in contrast to, e.g., ordinary linear programming, which has no such question at all. The answers provide insights into the general limitations we encounter compared with classical machine learning techniques when dealing with these representations. We also discuss, among other things, the limits on accuracy that this approach imposes. At some cases we show examples where certain sub-optimal solutions are obtained because our reconstruction algorithm cannot converge rapidly enough or reliably close the precision-vs.-accuracy trade-off between accuracy and computation time. Finally, we present new experimental results showing that even after several iterations the result remains rather stable under different statistical/statistical inference rules for our representations.

High-resolution segmenting of tooth crown fissures using nonparametric multivariate regularization for feature-free, polygonal, parametrical reconstruction. We propose a new method based on hierarchical linear algebra (HLA) that can be efficiently used to segment an entire tooth root. Instead of attempting to use a very high amount of parameters or gradient computation in order obtain optimal results, we directly incorporate the best features of individual teeth into our model in a parameter-dependent manner and leverage their overlap to achieve a good result. The resulting curve fit to each landmark provides a continuous indication on where to focus further investigation in terms of determining the most likely path to take (i.e. tooth edge). For this paper, we present experiments using state-of-the-art DRSM hardware with a sampling rate of 100 times per second, as well as several thousand data points collected over a period of weeks by multiple users over 8 consecutive days to evaluate the effectiveness of our method. Our procedure was confirmed after performing hundreds of thousands of steps per hour using real human and simulated teeth. While results were inconsistent (due to the variance inherent in sampling), they were statistically significant at p<0.001 when considering 10 000 steps/hour. Abstract The results of this study are presented to investigate whether a computational model based on spatial location could be used to represent the information in cross-modal progressive comprehension (CTP) tasks, one such example being that of referring language referred and categorized by reference words and phonemes with respect to an object. An approach involving using statistical learning methods rather than conventional regression methods had been proposed previously for CTP-like problems [1], but these types of methods have not yet been empirically applied. A hierarchical decision procedure is employed instead to determine which stimulus components provide the highest probability of representing the target word type during a given trial block, while simultaneously accounting for the effects of other elements in task configuration, context, and difficulty. Our preliminary evaluation suggests our method provides efficient representation of cue combinations over the whole set of trial blocks without sacrificing power or accuracy. As we progress further into implementation of our technique it should also prove possible to obtain higher accuracy/power across the different stages of the problem solving process where it may be most optimal. Additionally, it may prove easier to implement and learn our approach due to its modular structure, which allows simultaneous selection of features as needed at specific periods and phases of task design. Although we did not apply the approaches shown above in a real-life task and only simulated models were evaluated herein, much more work needs to be done before it can be considered for use directly as

Food image segments: A large-scale benchmark for food segmentations? The challenges involved in determining the number of distinct categories within processed items and food groups are considerable and require robust, large-scale analysis techniques. Here we use an independent sample of 30,000 images to determine the number of categories between those found with existing algorithmatology for each class. Our results show that the new food segmentation method achieves high accuracy while limiting overfitting when compared with prior approaches implemented for example by Google's ImageNet or Facebook's Caffe framework. When applied to large volumes of data such as photographs with many foods in various parts of the image, these methods yield significantly higher accuracies than have been previously observed. In combination with machine learning algorithms based on multiple layers of feature representation we achieve our largest performance wins yet: more than 2/3 of all categories detected remain under the most restrictive feature combinations even after training over 10 hours on a single GPU. This paper presents an implementation of logistic (black-box) shape model in deep neural networks, and shows that it has wide applicability in biomedical imaging as well as other fields where large numbers of objects are analyzed simultaneously using high rates of detection. We first describe our implementation of logistic (BLM) logistic shape models as the second generalization of non-parametric random field methods such as the Mixture Modeling approach on logistic regression, or SVM or RBF. We present multiple learning algorithms, each with different advantages based on their flexibility for fine tracking of local properties of the sampled data. For example, the kNN algorithm improves performance because you only need four parameters instead of 32; the Gaussian kernel network provides optimal inference on images where no noise can be removed from each frame by averaging over all possible positions; and the Random Graph Network achieves best overall results while minimizing overconnections among different neurons which may contribute error signals. Finally, we review the various features that make one logistic shape model or other unique compared to others and conclude that logistic shapes will make the next generation of biomedical image segmentation and analysis applications an even more exciting place to work. This should increase their potential impact globally since so much image processing involves this type of object recognition problem.

On May 28, 2014 at 3am PST, an email was sent by one of my students to me in regard to the upcoming Eureqa, a large data set that contains around 150 million geo-coded points in OpenStreetMap data. The data was acquired via Google Maps API and has been released by them as Open Data. The data was provided free of charge so that other users can mine to their own benefit. After learning about the new collection of this kind of mapping data created by Google, I wrote a small script that analyzed every point to obtain information on color, type and size of features in the image as well as position and orientation of the line between two points. Here are pictures of sample results where the lines have become blurred (in the case where one or more objects are in front of the camera): The resulting analysis of image segmentation showed significant patterns; there were some interesting findings concerning the relationships of certain types of feature which did not fit well with our known knowledge regarding natural scene construction and object definition techniques. In particular there exists a strong association between contour segments with sharp edges - such as water areas - and edge loops. And in general edge boundaries are associated with low resolution features like mountains whereas high quality ones look like valleys. As you can see in these images there are multiple peaks where contours follow straight paths while curves usually take many turns

Background An ideal neural network uses fully connected neurons to solve complex data processing tasks, and its models are widely studied for image and speech recognition in particular. However, it is also possible to represent a large amount and type of real world information from our everyday language experiences such as news articles, tweets or Facebook posts. We introduce NN, a general class of recurrent neural networks that is able to cope with any number of types of external information sources simultaneously without losing track or becoming overwhelmed by these different sources of input data. Using this architecture we present experiments on semantic segmentation using NNs from two different layers, based upon word embeddings extracted from a Twitter corpus and from an annotated text set of German translations. Experiments were performed on a COTS (Commercial Off The Shelf) Intel® Xeon Phi™ Coprocessor chip. Key Results On a single machine performance metrics indicate very high performances with one NN layer trained per machine on English corpora at around 2 M words per minute. On another machine we achieve around 6 TFLOPs by introducing many smaller NNs, making up 1 to 10 W of computation, into a larger CNN model by reclassifying sentences based upon their grammatical relevance during training. Evaluation shows that NNs train within seconds on both corpora which results is fast enough in comparison to traditional deep convolutional models and comparable to state of the art neural network architectures based on more conventional techniques such as support vector machines.

We propose an unsupervised algorithm based on gen_v2 (GenEvolution) that can be easily used anywhere in computer vision, where one wants to learn about objects by inferring their representations of other objects. The main idea is to have different classes of unknown object objects with arbitrary properties and then infer the features of these object models, either through direct evaluation or by using a model comparison toolbox that builds inference structures dynamically according to class constraints. Then the user asks how they expect each new learning step should look like, such as selecting the best fitting structure, comparing its quality against each potential alternative model or just giving some advice on which model he/she likes most and will probably use more often. This Research Paper introduces the use of semi-supervised learning to derive semantic segmentation models, which we compare both on tasks such as human translation (or machine interpretation) and natural language processing applications where there are both high levels of generalisation (i.e., in many cases the same output may appear more than once ) and low level prediction difficulties (a limited number or context free phrase matches in a corpus). We report results comparing four different semi-supervised learning approaches: unsupervised regularising, deep reinforcement learning, neural network based recurrent networks and an extended generative model that includes word embeddings. Our approach does only simple feature extraction with no feature selection and is based on a small family of convolutional layers used by traditional image classification systems. The main findings obtained show that semantically motivated classifiers have significantly lower error rates than previous state-of-the-Art solutions as well as faster convergence when solving larger and more complex problems (in various domains) while having higher performance on some tasks. This shows that an accurate and efficient semantic segmenting algorithm can be built without training labeled data – that is, it avoids having to compute features, preprocessed data through regularisations etc. Related news

Deep learning can help solve real-world problems by enabling algorithms with more accurate models that are less prone to human error and thus can be safer during real world usage that many other image recognition software solutions on these systems. However, deep neural nets (DNN) pose several challenges. They require extremely large amounts of data to train the network because they learn on a continuous basis as the model scales. Furthermore, even training a DNN requires extensive memory management to store all the inputs and activations made by a user that could potentially lead to loss if repeated. To address this issue, we propose to develop and validate a segmented (aka labeled) DNN to tackle the aforementioned issues. We use three approaches with different strengths when building our pipeline: linear classifier + convolutional networks; a modified version of a backpropagation algorithm based on gradient descent; and an attention based approach called attention control. Our system does not directly learn the images on the computer screen but instead uses its preprocessing steps to create label tags with which it assigns classes to pixels in generated images. These tag pairs may be used to produce additional information such as color or depth that could aid users and engineers on developing better ways to detect pneumothorapia in their patients. Automatic and direct methods for estimating subnetworks in large-scale dataset: A computational solution to suboptimal inference algorithms In this work, we present an automatic method proposed to augment self-guided learning (SG), using the direct algorithm or a non-supervised learning approach such as cross-guiding. Specifically, our general form of SG requires only 1 hidden layer with 2x2 output vector space and two hidden layers in order to solve a certain classification task that demands very few data iterations. This condition has been found empirically on a variety of datasets including real-world medical image segmentations, but also on relatively simple object labels presented on many different images. On these tasks it can often take longer than usual time to infer whether a given image belongs to our training datasets or not thanks to random data biases introduced by classifiers or by human interpretations made while judging the label of an image. In this context, this problem appears as one where there may be unanticipated information at the first sample, especially after introducing several new features into a neural net during training that are completely uncorrelated with each other, thus potentially adding noise to their resulting estimates (i.e., a model averaging). We present an adversarial reinforcement learning formulation and training protocol that extends our previous work on multi-Scale Autoencoders (MSACs) in two directions: first, we learn a system which can classify handwritten scribbles into the four groups shown above. This procedure will enable researchers seeking highly accurate segmentation algorithms to accelerate the existing MSAC methods by more than 20 percent compared to conventional methods. Second, it extends the current MSAC results substantially to multi scale models where more classes are learned simultaneously -- from scratch and with no prior knowledge about the input data. By building this algorithm from scratch, we have created a proof-of-concept design for scalable multiclass networks using the recent advances in machine learning techniques such as support vector machines and decision trees to efficiently achieve these goals. We show how the resulting framework uses a new architecture described by one of the original paper's authors to capture the features we need to train the model. These observations reveal how to construct hierarchical representations (LSTMs) to address the two main issues discussed earlier at the core of supervised attention processes: representation of features to simplify inference while avoiding overfitting; and selection process whereby our method maximizes classifier performance despite incomplete information. In addition to its novel approach on the segmentation problem, the LSTM framework provides exciting insight on other classification problems involving image feature extraction and detection. Unsupervised supervised learning in medical image segmentation – a review and an evaluation This paper presents a new unsupervised supervised learning algorithm based on contrast-based registration, which enables us to classify both high quality medical images and low resolution ones into several classes while making relatively simple predictions about each class according to individual features (in particular their spatial and color information). The performance and efficiency of this classifier can be evaluated by using a set of training/testing data that we obtained recently through automatic face detection as part of medical diagnosis. Our method has many advantages over previous deep learning methods such as support vector machine(svm), dropout, random forests, logistic regression, and convolutionsal neural networks. It uses less memory and is much faster than all these others. To perform comparisons with previous methods, the proposed implementation of the proposed pattern recognition algorithm in LCA was compared against two popular Deep Learning implementations to produce accurate labels at lower computational cost (from ~$1Kg CIFAR500 for 100 million feature+label pairs to $2M for 1 billion feature+label pair sequence) for both training sets (>100k label samples; 2 × 200 epochs per sample). Performance for small datasets shows similar but better results comparing the best performance in supervised classification (CVLTCH or SVM) techniques versus LCA, especially when the number of features increased. Abstract. We introduce machine learning techniques that can be used to learn to discriminate between two separate regions of images with similar features in view and find corresponding portion representations for those regions. Our new mechanism achieves on average 0.66% correct discrimination even when taking into account many additional noise sources, including different feature differences among views, shape differences related to size of objects to object contrast, etc., all taken together adding up to one's image recognition problem as expected. Further, our approach yields better overall performance than common approaches currently deployed on task such as classifying handwritten digits (e.g., [1]). This was achieved despite taking into account over 200 independent classes and 1000 data items each separated by different cross borders without any loss in accuracy. Furthermore, we obtain performance levels comparable with state-of-the-art methods as established by existing large scale machine learning competitions across domains including language and speech recognition, and object detection and categorisation. This work studies our approach to image segmentation under unsupervised learning and how that can be applied within a supervised framework. A simple model for automatic segmentation has been proposed [5], [10]. The objective function, which we call AUC (Area Under Curve), estimates the region where feature maps overlap; the size of this area is the distance between features along a projection unit. Our method aims at automatically determining the optimal projection units for each set feature map, in order to create classification classifiers based on different projected unit images. In particular, we have focused on designing a prototype alignment algorithm that can take an arbitrary biopsies into account while detecting tumor types. Such aligners are usually used for cancer detection models to extract the distribution of cells and their tissues during imaging such as using light or electron microscopy. The goal here is just making these machines more convenient—for example it does not matter what kind of tissue was taken from patient or even the actual location of tumor, because all it is doing if its mapping process, and thus determining the optimal projection unit. By developing a generic tool of using two dimensional projections with no special care about any point selection mechanism, we demonstrate fully unsupervised segmentation of biomicroscopic sections such as T4E8 (3D tumor outline) microcircles without prior knowledge of the morphology or other relevant information of the biopsy specimen [11], [12]. Using this machine learning technique, we were able to generate thousands of unique categories

Abstract: In this research, we present and discuss several methods that enable medical image segmentation with limited supervision when using deep neural networks. We address multiple common cases in real-world applications by evaluating different algorithms implemented on a set of existing open source software. Examples include using convnets coupled to the L2 convolution as proposed in our work to support high performance object recognition—a case explored previously in our paper with an example involving blood pressure measurement (Brenner et al., 2015) [pdf]. Using an edge detectors, our method can be used to detect object features based on shape and size of images that typically do not correspond well with human interpretations during medical imaging analysis. In contrast, several other methods focus more on object detection by finding edges. Here, in addition to using edge detectors, our method supports using feature maps captured during training or learning to infer feature representations of certain objects using very small numbers of units. Our approach leverages various concepts developed elsewhere in this and previous papers addressing image segmentation problems via convolutions (Kriegeskorte et al., 2009; Lu et al., 2016). These papers provide examples to explore different scenarios that are suitable for generalization. One important difference between our approaches and earlier works including our own is the use of machine learning techniques to solve the problem at hand, which makes them more practical, robust, and scalable in practice, while also avoiding many of the pitfalls that have prevented some practitioners adopting or deploying these techniques

Environmental Monitoring and Diagnostics Molecular imaging has changed considerably in recent years because of new techniques, improved image quality, advances in processing power and availability of data on whole organisms and sub-cellular structures. In particular, the resolution and quality of microscopy image data have continued to be improving rapidly (Schulz et al., 2010). There are now many open source tools for analyzing microarrays using various image processors such as ImageJ. This paper outlines an approach that provides access to environmental monitoring and diagnostic databases to obtain integrated molecular images for microbial biomass analysis. We developed this work based on four main concepts: 1) The use of large amounts of realtime DNA sequences, and their associated read mapping information, not only for the visualization but also for statistical, computational or biological methods. 2) We create a dataset with the highest number of sequenced individual bacteria/organisms representing all major phyla (e.g., Prokaryotes, Bacteria, Viruses, Spirochetes, Eukarya) at different time points over several months. Our goal was threefold: to show a possible utility in these techniques to measure biomasses at cellular, regional and continental scales; 3) To provide an easily accessible platform to present this dataset during high-end conferences, workshops, scientific meetings etc.; and 4) To extend existing knowledge about environmental microbiology by using novel images, in order to better understand environmental changes occurring on land-based samples in the environment. To achieve these goals, we used a large database comprising ~27 billion nucleotides generated from a total of 3976 bacteria/organisms represented over the
'On the theory of tournament type decision-making in the presence of uncertainty,' by A. Schmied, G. J. Nasser, R. A. Luthbeck, F. P. G. van den Hoexter, M. Hochholzer, J. C. J. A. Mollberg, W. J. Wigfrew, M. L. de Haan. Abstract: Tournament type decision-making allows individuals to estimate the utility of their opponent in a zero-sum tournament. We show that this optimal choice is robust to a number of potential sources of randomness, including the strength of the opponent, the relative probability of playing your particular style, and the probability of success of the strategy you chose. Furthermore, individuals can use this information to modify their optimal strategy in the face of uncertainty. To make sense of the results obtained here, the reader must know a bit of mathematics. Original Article: I like this paper and highly recommend everyone should read it. The title is "On the theory of tournament type decision-making in the presence of uncertainty." This paper discusses how the optimal strategy for a player in a tournament can change in the face of uncertainty. It will probably surprise many people to learn that this phenomenon is common in the real world where games have a finite amount of time. Abstract The goal of this work was to develop a new method for collaborative sparse regression (CSR) that uses spatially correlated support vectors (SVs). We propose a novel approach based on the use of a sparse matrix of SVs, which we call the Sparse Support Vector Matrix (SSVM), and a sparse vector space over it. Our method is able to perform CSR in a way that is more efficient than existing methods, while maintaining high accuracy. In particular, our method can be used to solve problems with large number of observations, such as those encountered when performing unmixing or clustering. This work also provides an introduction to the theory behind CSR, including its application to unsupervised learning tasks. This is a very interesting paper. I think it's important to note that the authors are not claiming that their method is better than other approaches. They're just saying that they've found a way to do it faster. That's all. It's not like they claim that their method is better than any other method out there. What they're doing is trying to find ways to make things run faster. And if you look at the results, they seem pretty good. So I'm going to give them credit for that. But I don't see how they could possibly have done anything else. Unsupervised Sparse Regression for Spatial Data Abstract: We present a model for sparse regression in the context of unsupervised spatial data. We learn a neural network, a linear kernel, using raw image data. This unsupervised algorithm enables the estimation of high-fidelity sparse kernels for the low-dimensional data, thereby achieving superior performance for the estimation of sparse and sparse-like kernels. We analyze the performance of this model on two challenging unsupervised datasets: 3-D spatio-temporal segmentation of images and 3-D spatio-temporal segmentation of time series. We observe that the sparse model is more accurate than random ensemble methods and provides the highest overall performance as compared to its unsupervised counterpart. Abstract The van der Waals force between two surfaces can be calculated by using the density functional theory (DFT) method to calculate the van der Waals forces between the surfaces. We have studied the effect of the surface topology on the van der Waals forces in bulk materials such as carbon nanotubes (CNTs), graphene, and diamond. In this work we show that the van der Waals force depends strongly on the surface topology. This result suggests that the van der Waals force may be used as an index of surface roughness for determining the surface roughness of bulk materials.

We present a new method to compute weighted Sobolev spaces (WS) on metric measure spaces, which can be used in many applications including classification and regression. We show that WSs are equivalent to regularized linear models with an additional parameter λ. The resulting WSs have a high degree of generality over regularized linear models, as well as being more efficient than their non-parametric counterparts. In particular, we find that WSs outperform regularized linear models when the number of parameters is large compared to the number of observations. Furthermore, we demonstrate that WSs can be efficiently computed using standard machine learning algorithms such as gradient descent and backpropagation. Our results also suggest that WSs may provide a promising alternative to the traditional approach to solving the problem of generating optimal classifiers based on the empirical distribution of data points.
We develop weighted Sobolev spaces on metric measure spaces. We show that weighted Sobolev spaces on metric measure spaces provide an efficient method to efficiently find the solutions of the equations of motion of a system of linear systems. We then show that their structure is independent of the type of metric space. We prove that, once an operator is introduced that defines a weighted Sobolev space, then the Sobolev-spaces of a given metric measure space are similar to the Sobolev-spaces of the metric measure space on which it is introduced. The resulting Sobolev-spaces are equivalent to those of the metric measure space on which the Sobolev-space operator is introduced. While Sobolev spaces have been studied for some time, for this particular paper we had to develop our own approach. The reason is simple: the space of weights on a metric measure is a continuous space, while a linear system is a discrete function, so a Sobolev system has both continuous and discrete components. Another thing to keep in mind is that a linear system is a vector space, and a Sobolev space a dual space. All linear systems arise as dual spaces between two functions. That is, a line function from the origin to a point is an operator on a linear system, as it changes values continuously. The result is that any metric measure such as [a n+b]×[a n+c] (where a n denotes the position number inside the system) is a dual space to the space of coordinates

The main advantages of the mathematical formula are: The structure is defined by vertices which are connected. We do not have to choose an arbitrary ordering among the vertices. The vertices are ordered by their properties The formula is valid for any number of structure types, all structures can be viewed as a single entity. We can also create a mathematical theory of structure. Our result is also valid for any point in the 3-D space. The formula is well understood by mathematicians. Indeed, a number of mathematicians have discovered the formula, and we have to thank them for it. The formula is based on the structure of the three-dimensional cube. The formula defines three basic vertices "point" (x,y,z), "line" (r,g,b) and "plane" (v,w,x,y,z). In this work, we present an experimental study on ion transport in macroscopic RF linear traps. We demonstrate that the ion transport is not limited by the size of the trap and can be achieved with a single-layer trap. The results show that the ion transport is dominated by the electric field gradient between the trapping surface and the trapped ions. This result suggests that the ion transport may be used as a method to measure the electric field gradients in the environment.
Background Recent observations of high-energy neutrinos in linear RF traps have presented strong evidence for the existence of a novel type of neutrino. The experimental challenge is to quantify the neutrino energy spectrum, since this property has significant application in neutron astronomy. The neutrino energy spectrum is quantitatively derived from the average spectrum of RF-induced neutrinos, with detailed measurements of various parameters used to constrain the spectrum to extremely high precision. A key difficulty in constraining the neutrino energy spectrum to relatively high precision is the extremely high background energy at the detector. However, low background energy can be resolved over time, to a similar degree of precision as has thus far been achieved for neutrino interactions. Because the same background energy was used to both constrain both neutrino energy decays and, more recently, provide a reference to quantitatively interpret the neutrino energy spectrum, it will be possible to perform an analysis of the neutrino spectrum at multiple facilities simultaneously. In this context, measurements of the neutrino energy spectrum of the linear trap are crucial to the understanding of the nature and origin of high-energy neutrinos. Methodology Four traps were constructed from a linear polarizer, a 50-μm band gap magnet, a 5-μm narrow bandgap magnet, and a linear RF source. Electrons were emitted from each magnet by a linear trap using an electron gun, which is a non-polarized source that produces the full ~15°×4°×2° beamwidth. From the captured electrons and protons the trap was built according to the simple principle
Flux braiding is an emerging technique that uses a laser to excite and then de-excite a single electron in a semiconductor material, which can be used as a source of light or as a detector. The technique has been applied to several materials including silicon, germanium, gallium arsenide (GaAs), indium phosphide (InP) and yttrium aluminum garnet (YAG). In this work we present a comprehensive analysis of the experimental results obtained with GaAs and YAG. We show that the method works well on both materials and that it provides good performance in terms of photon energy conversion efficiency. Furthermore, we demonstrate that the method can be used to study the quantum mechanical properties of the electrons in these two materials.
Flux braiding is an approach to shaping a periodic structure by weaving together alternating layers of carbon material, with the result that material density and structure changes over an entire period of time. This approach has recently been developed for use by nanostructured structures, such as photovoltaic devices and carbon nanotube microfluidic channels, and is currently being widely used in the solar industry for production of highly porous materials (e.g., SiO2 and Al2O3). The process has also attracted much attention from industry in the form of flexible and lightweight material composites, where the carbon particles are interconnected together, both physically as by fibres and in software as by a variety of techniques. This paper presents a review of some of the key theoretical and experimental findings for flux braiding. More detail on the experiments from this and previous work can be found below. Our analysis focuses on the most recent literature on the topic of flux braiding with the aim of providing an overview of the current results. We present an analysis of the consistency of the standard model of particle physics with asymptotic-safe quantum gravity, which we call the Asymptotically Safe Quantum Gravity (ASQG) framework. We show that the ASQG framework can be used to derive a consistent theory of quantum gravity in terms of the Standard Model and its extensions. The ASQG framework allows us to make predictions about the behavior of particles under conditions where they are not affected by any external perturbations. These predictions are then tested against observations made using experiments on the LHCb experiment at CERN. Our results indicate that the ASQG framework provides a robust foundation for future studies of quantum gravity.
Quantum gravity is widely considered a necessary condition for the existence of a theory of quantum mechanics that is consistent with observation. This consistency condition has no theoretical justification, since there exists no experimentally testable quantum theory of gravity that is consistent with observation. In this paper, we describe a new quantum gravity model which satisfies such a requirement; in contrast with previous models that are inconsistent, this model shows no evidence of violating the requirement of being consistent with observation. Our model has numerous advantages over previous models; we address the problem of how to model the behavior of massive objects in space, thus showing that quantum gravity is not a general requirement for the consistency of non-relativistic theories, but is restricted to the problem of describing behavior of massive objects in spacetime.
Abstract Anisotropy in the magnetization of anisotropic compounds with a single-electron bond, such as uranyl(OH) 2, has been studied using a variety of techniques including Raman spectroscopy and X-ray diffraction. We study the effect of the shape of an attractor on its parameters, i.e., the number and distribution of points in the network. We find that the shape of the attractor affects the number of points in the network by increasing the average number of points per node. This increase in the average number of points per node leads to a decrease in the mean number of nodes per point. The decrease in the mean number of nodes per point causes a reduction in the variance of the network. These effects are due to the fact that the number of nodes increases with the square root of the number of points. In addition, we show that the shape of the attractor also influences the distribution of the points within the network. Specifically, we find that the shape of the attractor has a strong influence on the probability density function (PDF) of the network. The PDF of the network is defined as the ratio between the number of nodes and the total number of points in the network. Our results suggest that the shape of the attractor can be used to improve the performance of parameter-dependent oscillatory networks.

Abstract The spin-squeezing effect (SSE) has been studied extensively by many groups over the past decades. In this work we study the SSE in two driven, dissipative, nonlinear cavities coupled with single and two-photon exchange. Polyhedral symmetry breaking in the plane In this work, we consider the case where an arbitrary polyhedron has no symmetries and is oriented along a single axis. We show that if one considers only the vertices of the polyhedron, then it can be broken into two pieces by O-minimal Hauptvermutung (OMH). This breaks the polyhedron into two pieces with the same number of vertices as the original polyhedron. The resulting pieces are orthogonal to each other and have the same orientation. In addition, they share all their edges. Thus, OMH allows us to break any polyhedron into two pieces without having to worry about the orientation of its vertices. We prove that OMH is always possible for any polyhedron with no symmetries. For example, we show that OMH is not possible for a cube with three faces. Furthermore, we show that OMH is also not possible for a tetrahedron with four faces. Finally, we show that OMH is not possible for a dodecahedron with six faces. This result shows that OMH is a generalization of the well known O-Minimal Hausdorff theorem.

To demonstrate "O-minimal Hauptvermutung for polyhedra II," 2D polyhedra of 3D with symmetric facets and faces are rotated by only 45 degrees. Furthermore, 2D polyhedra of 4D with faces are rotated by only 135 degrees and 3D polyhedra of 5D with faces are rotated by only 130 degrees. At O-minimal-minimal, as in the previous research, the faces of the polyhedra do not rotate. Finally, for both sets of polyhedra, the faces and facets with the largest and smallest faces do not rotate at all. In the paper, O-minimal is described in terms of a single rotation function that is the same for all the polyhedra. The concept of O-minimal was coined in the 1930s by mathematician William G. Lawvere, who observed that many simple polyhedra have vertices for which they appear to have two equal and opposite faces. He proposed the first formal theorem on this phenomenon: "if there is an O-minimal of this form for a group of polyhedra, then there is a special instance of it for all polyhedra." The theorem was independently discovered by physicist Paul Gross (1911-2007) and mathematician Thomas E. Lawvere. O-minimal for polyhedra has two important effects. Abstract: For many years, the ability to view, analyze, and visualize information with images has been hampered by the need to process and convert images from one spectrum view into another For example, it can be challenging to convert data from the Infrared (inf) and infrared/visible (ir/v) channels of an electromagnetic spectrum image into an equivalent spectrum channel from the microwave frequency (MW) waveband (blue) to the infrared/IR/visible (ir/v) channel from a standard image data stream. Since there are many common imaging techniques, most of which share the same spectral characteristics, the task of converting from one to the other can become inefficient. This can be very problematic for imaging applications. For example, if the IR spectrum of the imager is too narrow to enable the detection of fluorescence, it is a problem to convert it to the visible spectrum. Likewise, the visible spectrum of the imager is too wide to make the detection of a pulsing color image, resulting in an inefficient use of the spectrum data. GQTC allows the user to select, to convert, or to blend images from various spectrum channels or bands, to perform a variety of image analysis, and to create spectra images. " This paper seeks to characterize and estimate the accuracy of the third gravitational-lensing test performed in 2013 by the ATLAS and CMS experiments. GREAT3 consists of a series of five searches and each has a different precision. The accuracy of any single measurement is dependent upon the precision of the remaining measurements (sigma), so estimating the precision of GREAT3 requires both estimating sigma and using the entire set of measurements made during GREAT3. The test was performed on data from six quasars, in order to reduce the sampling overhead. The results are interpreted as an upper (true) precision of.0001 ± 1%, which is approximately 0.0005% less than the previous highest value (0.0004%). The precision estimate is biased downward, but is significantly downward, given the lack of knowledge of how GREAT3 compares to previous tests of this kind. This error has significant implications for the interpretation of the new results from the new gravitational-lensing efforts. Given that one of these new experiments is the W. M. Keck Observatory and the other is the Fermi Telescope, the results will affect the public understanding of the data and, thus, of the capabilities of these astronomical observatories. We hope that this report can help to better communicate and encourage collaboration between the two observatories."
Abstract We present a new approach to the problem of backtracking in quantum mechanics, which we call B\"acklund transformations (B-trees). We show that B\"acklund transforms are consistent with classical physics, and that they can be used to solve many problems in quantum information theory. The main idea behind B\"acklund transformations is that one can use them to find the shortest path between two points on an arbitrary graph. This allows us to perform computationally efficient backtracking operations, such as finding the minimum spanning tree or maximum spanning tree. In particular, we prove that B\"acklund transforms satisfy the standard quantum algorithm for computing the minimum spanning tree, and that they also satisfy the standard quantum algorithm for computing the maximum spanning tree.
1 Introduction The world of quantum mechanical systems is currently in the process of revolutionizing the way we view physical phenomena. It is not only that the fundamental nature of matter has been overturned in a huge variety of ways, but also that quantum mechanics describes a new, "quantum" way of communicating information, in which an entangled signal (the "b\"acklund") can be transformed into the original, non-entangled, signal (the "w\"acklund"). These transformations allow the production of a new class of entangled quantum states that has been termed "b\"acklund transforms" (1). The notion of "quantum state" used here takes on three distinct forms (Fig. 1): (i) the macroscopic, thermodynamic or electrostatic quantum state, which refers to the classical properties of matter (for which the B-state and the Z-state are most familiar); (ii) the quantum mechanical quantum state, which is encoded in the system as a quantum mechanical operator with a well-defined and fully quantum mechanical description; and (iii) the wave function, which describes one version of the macroscopic quantum state. The first three forms are the familiar classical forms of the classical quantum state. This paper focuses on the fourth, wave function, representing the quantum mechanical state encoded in the operator. The B-state and Z-state are the most well-known classes of quantum properties, and are therefore the areas of study for many types of research. These quantum properties constitute a set of possible states of a physical system consisting of particles, atoms or molecules, or of a quantum system in general.
Abstract The resonant optical modes of Au (III) triangular nanoantennas have been studied using spectroscopic techniques, but their emission spectra are not well characterized. Here we report on the characterization of these modes by using an excitation laser with a wavelength of 532 nm and an imaging laser with a wavelength of 740 nm. We observe that the resonance frequency of the mode at the center of the antenna is about 1/3 of the resonance frequency of the mode in the outer ring. This result indicates that the resonant optical modes can be excited by light with wavelengths shorter than those used to excite them.

Abstract: This paper presents a compact and active-control-compatible way of designing and controlling resonant mode-splitting in a narrow cavity. Through the use of a phase-converted resonant cavity of resonant frequency, a set of simple geometric resonant modes are transformed into modes whose spectrum corresponds to a single spectral frequency. These modes are designed to split the vibrating acoustic radiation into a resonance frequency corresponding to a single wavelength. The spectrum is then amplified multiple times by a photodiode on some light emitting surface such as a semiconductor film. In many cases, the amplifier can be adjusted to produce frequencies with a tunable and controllable center frequency. The mode-splitting efficiency is therefore very high. Figure 4: One type of design of a resonant microcavity that features a spectral mode splitting approach. In the example shown here, the optical beam has been filtered as a function of the incident mode-splitting wavelength. As can be seen, the spectrum emitted by a resonator with a single spectral mode is much narrower than the spectrum emitted by a resonator whose resonant frequencies reflect the modes of an ensemble of multiple resonators. Abstract The problem of finding an invariant in a non-linear system can be solved by using a groupoid, which is a set of points that are connected by a linear transformation. In this work we consider a differentiable groupoid with two components, one of which is a vector space over the real numbers. We show that the de Rham theorem implies that the invariance of the groupoid is equivalent to the invariance of the vector space over the real numbers. This result has important implications for the study of dynamical systems, including the theory of inertia.

It is shown that the standard de Rham theorem fails to satisfactorily satisfy a number of test cases for the discrete-dimensional case when the density function is fixed at nonzero values. In contrast, the differential de Rham theorem is not violated under any of the other two extreme de Rham approximations." We present an algorithm that can detect violations of the majority rule in software programs, and we show how it can be used to detect violations of other kinds of rules such as those governing memory allocation or synchronization. We also describe several applications of our technique, including detecting violations of the Java programming language's memory model, detecting violations of the C++ standard library's memory model, and detecting violations of the Java virtual machine's memory model. Our approach has been implemented on the JVM and runs on both x86-64 and ARM architectures. The results are promising, with detection rates up to 99% for some classes of violations.
Detecting Missing Method Calls as Violations of the Majority Rule Introduction The majority rule is a foundational assumption in machine learning (MC) that states that a successful algorithm must be effective. While this assumption has great promise, there have been many questions raised about how to actually derive and generalise from the majority rule. The purpose of this paper is to explore and formalise how to formally model the majority rule. We begin by exploring the case when an algorithm (i) makes multiple calls to the minimizer and (ii) does not terminate because of missing (missing) calls. We describe the derivation of the rules of thumb for a simple (but inefficient) algorithm known as minimax that considers the number of missing calls made on its input and makes further calls. In addition, we give evidence supporting the correctness of this algorithm by analyzing the case where it made many call, but did not make the right number and did not terminate (because it did not terminate). We then describe the generic class on which the minority rule is based and introduce the Minority Rule. This generic rule allows us to represent the possibility that an algorithm may miss a call to avoid termination of its execution, as an alternative to the majority rule (that ignores this possibility). We also give further evidence supporting the correctness of the Minority Rule by using this generic representation to demonstrate the safety of the algorithm. Abstract We study the extreme properties of two game theoretic models, one based on the Flooding Game and the other on the Prisoner's Dilemma. We find that both models exhibit an interesting property known as 'extremality', which we term 'extreme extremity'. Extreme extremity arises when the probability of winning in a given game depends on the outcome of previous games. In particular, it occurs when the expected value of winning in a given game depends on the outcomes of all previous games. This result has important implications for understanding the dynamics of cooperation and competition in social dilemmas.
In this paper, we present two new empirical results concerning the extreme properties of two flood-filling games. In First-order FloodFilling (F1-F2) (1,F1), flood-filling a box can't happen if more than x number of cards are present. But in Extreme FloodFilling 2 (E2-E3), flood-filling a box doesn't happen if more than y numbers of cards are present, if some other card is also present. Further, we show that the same phenomena occur in the same two scenarios with different probabilities of occurrence. We tested these results against different card distributions in two large, two-player variants: a simplified version and one with two players. Our results indicate that flood-filling and extreme flood-filling are similar. Furthermore, we show that an extreme situation does not always need to generate an extreme outcome. The full source code for the paper is available here: http://arxiv.org/abs/arXiv:1712.04973
We present an algorithm that can be used to efficiently trade on the WIG20 futures market, which trades at a price per contract of $0.01. The algorithm exploits the fact that the underlying asset (WIG20) has a finite supply and that it is possible to buy or sell contracts with a single transaction. We show that this approach is optimal when the number of available contracts is small compared to the total amount of money that will ever be invested in the market. Our results suggest that the algorithm is able to outperform other strategies such as the Black Swan method, which uses the same basic idea but does not take into account the possibility of large future returns.
Zipfian statistics are frequently used in the decision sciences to analyze complex systems. Some examples are the distribution of stock returns in large markets, the dynamics of stock markets, the dynamics of the weather, and the behavior of stocks. Because the Zipf distribution is a non-negative probability density function, it has been applied to many real-life phenomena, such as natural disasters and stock market fluctuations. The Zipf distribution of expected stock returns is particularly suited to handle long-term stock market forecasts for two reasons. First, the distribution is not simply normal; the probability function is not symmetric with respect to length. There can be high probabilities (p < 0.01) for long returns, for small p, and for large p. The Zipf distribution of return expectation is a "flat" distribution, which is often used for smooth distributions in finance [Kahneman, 1979]. Second, the distribution of return expectation for a stock price depends heavily on the correlation of returns to the market-moving events (i.e., correlation and correlation-momentum). In the case of the stock market, there is a direct way to identify the short-term value strategy of the investor. The correlation of prices is the key predictor of long-term returns for stocks [Kahneman and Tversky, 1979; Dufour and Kahneman, 1979]. In that paper, however, the results on the correlation of returns to the market were not very strong. Here, by using the distribution of the expected return on WIG20 futures, the result reached significance (p < 0.05), allowing a more intuitive understanding of
Abstract: The purpose of this work was to model and semigroup formulate the ACL beam in terms of its electrostatic, quasi-static, and fully dynamic properties. Our results show that the semi-classical approximation does not provide good approximations to the real system. However, it provides good approximations to the quasi-classical approximation. This means that the quasi-classical approximation can be used as a basis for further modeling and semigroup formulation of the ACL beam.

Abstract The electronic structure of MoS 2 has been studied extensively, but its electronic properties are not well understood. Here we report an electrochemical reaction between MoS 2 and a metal-free substrate (MoS 2 /TiO 2 ) at room temperature. We demonstrate that the MoS 2 /TiO 2 interface exhibits a large number of open pores, which can be exploited to access the MoS 2 surface with high efficiency. Two-qubit quantum states are widely used to represent information about physical systems, such as atoms and molecules. However, two-qubit quantum states have been shown to be highly sensitive to local quantum uncertainty. Here we show that this sensitivity can be exploited by exploiting the fact that the state of an atom or molecule is not only a function of its position but also of its spin. We demonstrate that the spin-spin coupling between two qubits can be used to encode information about the local quantum uncertainty of the system. This allows us to perform measurements on the system without affecting the state of the system at all. The results suggest that two-qubit quantum states may provide a promising platform for quantum information processing applications.

Abstract Chaining, interpolation, and convexity are three important properties that characterize the shape of a polyhedron. We present an algorithm to compute these properties in a general way, which we call chain-based convexity. Chain-based convexity is based on the idea that each vertex has a unique set of edges connecting it to all other vertices. The algorithm computes the convex hull of this graph by iteratively applying the chain rule to each edge. This results in a convex hull with a single root node at every vertex. In addition, the algorithm can be used to generate new graphs with different shapes. Our approach is inspired by the work of [Klein]. We show how to use our method to solve problems involving convex hulls, such as the problem of generating a convex hull of a sphere or torus.

Together, these techniques allow to build highly scalable and robust multilayer convolutional neural networks, often with thousands of layers. Abstract The social force model (SFM) has been used to explain the dynamics of human migration and its effects on societies, but it does not provide an explanation of why people migrate. We show that the SFM can be modified so as to account for this missing information by introducing a new parameter called the'social cost' which captures the costs associated with moving. This modification allows us to predict how much migrants will move when they are faced with different levels of uncertainty about their future welfare. Our results suggest that the social cost should increase as society becomes more uncertain about its future welfare. In particular, we find that the social cost increases as the level of uncertainty increases. These findings have important implications for understanding the causes of migration and for policy making.
Evacuation by the social force model is often seen as a permanent equilibrium response to the environment where all individuals are fully able to escape. We develop this notion as a hypothesis for the social force model and find that it is highly sensitive to the amount of time between the initial equilibrium and the first moment of escape, suggesting that, for the entire system, there is a limit to how long each individual can avoid escape from the social force. We show that the limiting parameters are the same even for highly mobile and mobile individuals. Furthermore, we show that the limit is independent of whether individuals are moving by themselves or relying on the interaction between the individuals. Our framework also allows us to explore the dynamics of the social force model with no initial condition, showing that the parameters change depending on the initial conditions of the model. The results show that social forces are more likely to evolve along social interactions rather than individual mobility, which shows that the social force model is not stationary. This paper is in response to another work which is entitled "Evacuation in the social force model is not stationary" by Robert G. Brown (2013). This paper finds that some of the initial conditions in the social force model can cause the model to end up with extremely unstable equilibria where large numbers of people might escape at any moment even if the others don't flee. We present a new algorithm, MUSIC (MUSIC-based estimation), which can be used to estimate the probability that a given music file will be played by a human listener. The algorithm uses a novel approach to estimating the probability of a particular piece of music being played by a human listener. We show how this method can be applied to many different types of data and we demonstrate its performance on several real world datasets. Our results indicate that MUSIC outperforms other methods in terms of accuracy and speed.
This article argues that a faster MUSIC DoA estimator is needed to be useful in music classification. Specifically, for a given class we expect the MUSIC and DoA parameters to be equal, but this does not hold for an arbitrary class of samples. In order to be equal, the MUSIC parameter needs to be better than the number of samples in the training set. Furthermore, the DoA parameter needs to be better than the number of samples in the test set. We first propose a new estimator, MINTAN1, which approximates a DoA value (MINTAN1(X + R)), which is better than the sum of the DoA values for the specified class of samples with R-1 samples. The MINTAN1 estimator outperforms the previous method, MUSICV1. MINTAN1 additionally outperforms MUSICV2 in the class of unbounded samples with R-2 samples, despite the larger number of samples required to compute the MUSIC estimator.
