In this work, we propose an approach to simultaneous inference that can be used to infer the structure and properties of multivariate functions in a variety of domains. We use a novel technique called'multimodel' to combine multiple independent models into one model with a single parameter. The resulting model has a high degree of independence between its parameters, which allows us to perform simultaneous inference on it. Our method is based on the idea that the joint probability distribution over all possible values of a function's parameters should be independent of each other. This means that if we have two different models, one with a given set of parameters and another with a different set of parameters, then the joint probability distribution over their parameters must also be independent. In our case, we are interested in finding the joint probability distribution over the joint parameters of a multivariate function. To do so, we first define a new class of multivariate functions called'multi-parameter functions'. These functions are defined by having at least three parameters (the number of observations, the number of features, and the number of classes). Then, we construct a new class of multi-parameter functions called'multimodel functions', which are functions whose parameters are not constrained to be multiples of 3. Finally, we show how to apply our multimodel inference algorithm to the joint probability distributions of these two classes of functions. Our results demonstrate that multimodel inference provides a powerful tool for solving problems involving multivariate functions.

Abstract The spectral gap property of the unitary group is one of the most important results in number theory, and it has been known since the early days of the field that this property holds for any commutative algebraic structure. However, there are many other interesting questions about the spectral gap property which have not yet been answered. Abstract We present a new approach to solving the Cauchy-Schwarz problem, which we call the Cauchy-Schwarz algorithm (CSAT). We show that CSAT can be used to solve any nonlinear system with a finite number of variables, including the Dirichlet process. Abstract The objective of this study was to compare the performance of a data-fit generalized Aw-Rascle-Zhang (GZ) model with an existing, well-known and widely used statistical method for predicting the probability of a given outcome in a multivariate regression analysis. We compare the model against two leading data-fitted generalized activation functions, including the Gaussian activation functions, the Naive Bayes activation function (a.k.a. the sigmoid), and the Fisher–Netravali function. We then compare the performance of the model with two different non-parametric estimators: the non-parametric mean absolute error (MAC) estimator and the parametric mean absolute error (MAPE) estimator. The MAPE estimator is based on the mean of the residuals over the error gradients from the non-parametric estimator. The data we use in the evaluation paper includes over 1,300,000,000 training examples. Our experimental results demonstrate that our Aw-Rascle-Zhang model is not only competitive, but slightly faster than several data-fitted neural network methodologies. Abstract: The quantum efficiency enhancement (QE) of high index nanowire resonator (HIR) devices has been studied extensively, but the QE of HIRs with low index (LIR) resonators remains unknown. Here we report on the QE of LIR resonators using a novel method that exploits the nonlinearity of the resonant frequency and the non-uniformity of the resonant cavity geometry to improve the QE. We demonstrate that the QE of LIR resonators can be enhanced by an order of magnitude compared to that of HIRs with similar resonant frequencies. This result indicates that the QE of LIR resonators may have applications beyond those of HIRs.

Abstract The 4-manifold Möbius strip is a common and common-sense solution to the boundary problem of a set of N vertices. Given the Möbius strip, we show that for a finite number of vertices, the number of ways to arrange a 4-dimensional array on N axes is given by the number of possible Möbius strips on the space of all pairs of such vertices. Abstract The time dependent variational principle (TDP) states that the probability of observing an event depends on its past history, which can be described by a sequence of discrete events. This paper presents a new approach to solving this problem using a state-of-the-art quantum algorithm called coherence or state-space orbitals. We show how to solve the TDP in a nonlinear way, i.e., we do not require any prior knowledge about the past histories of the system. Our method allows us to solve the TDP with a single qubit, without requiring any additional information about the initial conditions of the system. In particular, our method does not depend on the choice of the initial conditions. We also demonstrate that our method can be used to solve other problems involving time dependent probabilities.

Abstract: The quantum world is characterized by an entanglement between particles that can be measured and manipulated, but which cannot be directly observed or controlled. This has led to the development of many models of quantum systems, including superconducting qubits, which are used as quantum computers. However, these models have been unable to reproduce the behavior of real-world systems such as photons, electrons, and atoms. Here we present a new approach to modeling quantum systems based on a single entangled photon. We show how this approach allows us to describe the dynamics of a system with a high degree of accuracy, even when it is not interacting with any other system. Our results demonstrate that our method provides a general framework for describing quantum systems at the macroscopic scale.
In principle there is a fundamental incompatibility between the wave form of a quantum system and the information needed for its observation. This is known to be especially apparent when such matter is in the form of entangled or entangled particles. The state space of these systems consists of a set of spin(3n) groups that are connected by a single spin(3n) group. In particular, we consider the case where the spins are all on the same side of the y axis. This leads to an interesting question: how can one measure the state of a system if it has no spin? To answer this question, we introduce a new concept called a spin(3n) group. A spin(3n) group is a collection of spin(3n) groups that have the same number of neighbors as their total number of neighbors. For example, the spin(3n) group {1, 2, 3} has three neighbors, so there are four possible states of the system. If we take the spin(3n) group {1, 2, 3}, then its spin(3n) group contains only two spin(3n) groups, which means that the spin(3n) group has only two spin(3n) neighbors. Thus, the spin(3n) group has only two spin(3n) neighbors. However, if we take the spin(3n) group {1, 2, 3}, then its spin(3n) group contains three spin(3n) groups, so there are five possible states of the system. Therefore, the spin(3n) group has six spin(3n) neighbors. We show that the spin(3n) group states in quantum circuits are determined by the spin(3n) group states in the corresponding spin(3n) group. Abstract The Nernst-Lifshitz free energy conjecture states that any non-trivial closed system with an arbitrary number of interacting particles can be described by a single, unique Hamiltonian. This conjecture has been proven in many different systems and has been used to prove many important results in physics. However, it remains open whether this conjecture holds for all systems or only certain classes of them. In this work we show that the Nernst-Lifshitz free energy conjecture does hold for all systems, which includes both classical and quantum systems. We also show that the Nernst-Lifshitz free energy conjecture holds for all classes of systems, including those where the Hamiltonian is not a linear combination of the Lagrangians. Finally, we present a proof of the Nernst-Lifshitz free energy conjecture using the standard quantum mechanics approach. This is a very interesting result, as it shows that the Nernst-Lifshitz free energy conjecture is true for all systems, even if they are not classical (or quantum) systems. It's also nice to see that the Nernst-Lifshitz free energy conjecture holds for all classes of systems, including ones where the Hamiltonian is not a linear combination of the Lagrangians. "The Nernst Theorem shows that a special kind of electromagnetic wave or quantum field is a model of spacetime. It is an example of a "spare model" in quantum field theory (QFT), which in turn is the case for all of quantum philosophy. The general properties of this wave (the vacuum, wave function or the electric matrix) can be used to construct an appropriate test. In particular, the Casimir-Lifshitz free energy (CLEF) test allows to understand the free energy of the empty space, the CLCFT or the CLCFT in the vacuum. This is also an important test for the interpretation of the special character of the vacuum. In the paper we present a test of the CLEF test for the QFT. The key concept is that the gravitational potential energy (GPE) for the empty space or of the vacuum (not of matter) is equal to the gravitational potential energy for an electromagnetic field. With this result we prove the Nernst Theorem." Abstract We developed a method for graphically constructing a large ensemble of systems for quantum error correction. With this method, we can construct and verify a large number of quantum error-correcting systems using only standard software and computational resources. To build that large ensemble, we built a high-performance simulation of quantum-error-correction systems that produces small clusters with high probability. All the quantum-error-correction algorithms developed so far have been single-objective approaches, relying, for example, on classical and classical-like tools for solving systems and simulation. Our framework instead gives a comprehensive set of tools for dealing with many complex systems, and the computational resources it require is a fraction of those needed for single-objective approaches, which simplifies the creation of such systems. Furthermore, our method does not impose any requirement to explicitly specify the solutions that a system must use, a prerequisite to many single objective methods. Our approach, therefore, not only provides a systematic approach to correcting quantum error, but also a generic strategy for designing and verifying a large number of quantum error-correction methods in software. Spin-orbit coupling between two baryonic molecules has been studied extensively, but it remains unclear whether this phenomenon can be observed in nonbaryonic systems. Here we report on the observation of spin-orbit coupling between a single molecule and an isolated spin-orbit system in a gas of boron atoms. The experiment was performed using a spin-orbital magnetic field (SOMF) generated by a laser pulse at a frequency of 1 MHz. We observe that the molecular orbital angular momentum (MO) of the atom is strongly correlated with its spin angular momentum (SO), which is also strongly correlated with the SO of the surrounding gas. This correlation leads to a strong interaction between the MO and the SO, which results in a significant change in the spin orbitals of both molecules. Our experimental results show that the observed spin-orbit coupling is due to the presence of a large number of spin-orbit pairs in the gas.

In The Two Noble Kinsmen, two men—both of whom were close personal friends of William Shakespeare—were involved in an apparently petty conflict in which they had the sole use of their bodies to perform acts of violence that affected each other and society itself. At the same time, one of them also engaged fully with the wider world, becoming a prominent writer as well as a leader in the political life of New England. Though this novel has a more straightforwardly humorous edge than most works of literature, in describing the circumstances surrounding these violent incidents—the origins of their quarrels, when one man ended his life, what kind of friendship they enjoyed—it can still be read as an analysis and examination of how we humans interact with our societies." In this book, we will see how we can use open source technology to build revolutionary tools that are completely self-sufficient and free, so as not to depend on 'the old system' so much anymore. For example, rather than having "software" like Office or Windows installed, many programs rely heavily on Open Systems Libraries (OSLs). As an OSL describes software it doesn't need user approval but you do have to download and install your very own versions if you choose to run one. Also OSLs aren't built or maintained by Microsoft and thus they all work quite differently because no single company runs both an operating system and its libraries. Furthermore, OSL also share parts and functions with standard library which means that the libraries are constantly changing and newer versions are often already out there. This means you could theoretically write a program that isn't dependent on any particular version...and then simply redistribute said program when there's been enough changes! In fact, there has recently been some great progress made towards achieving this where OLS can be run in a separate process called COSA. So while software remains mostly proprietary and we still get stuck using Office 2012 or Apple Safari 5, we've got things pretty good at least from a software perspective right? This sounds almost too good to be true, however, given a little bit more thought, the facts behind such a claim become clear enough. Firstly, software dependency in these modern times largely comes from companies

'Giant's bread" (also known (by British settlers) as 'Big Ben') was an enormous British bread in London, and its distinctive shape, size, colouring and taste attracted attention and gained it a reputation in its own right. The legend goes that it was invented on 1 February 1765 by Henry Woodhouse, brother-in-law to Sir Joseph Banks, founder of the Bank of England. In his early career Henry Woodhouse sold sweets at Mr. Pickering's candy shop before taking on other, more lucrative enterprise: the preparation and wholesale sale of breakfast meat patties which he called big sandwiches or pate on roll. As a result of his success with huge bread he decided on a scheme which would prove highly profitable, both commercially and socially: he introduced the Big Sandwich (an exact copy of his sandwich which he had eaten so often whilst selling sweets) to London for distribution throughout 1818[12]. The idea did not take off however – when word got around of a giant roll containing one hundred slices of bread, each weighing two pounds, prices soared; as a cost effectiveness measure many people decided to use them as large biscuits which were then cut up for consumption at home.[13] Unfortunately this didn't exactly work out as planned. By 1870 Big Ben's profits were much lower than expected and people began using the bread to braid ropes (not exactly unusual within Britain). To cater for this demand for rope braid, many pubs began serving gin cocktails while also selling large rolls

His fictionalizations of native communities and cultures have frequently been called "disgracesable." About The Author Arthur C. Clarke (6th March 1921 – 31st January 2016) was an American writer and sci-fi author credited with more than fifty films, many which have become iconic. Clarke's body of work includes such works as 2001 and A Journey in Starshine, including his seminal short story "That Which Survives". His science fiction writings included such titles Asimov's Fave Stories; Time Enough for Dreaming; The City Trilogy; Darkest Hours; Childhood's End; and Rendezvous With Rama or Return To Earth. He adapted his first feature screenplay into Star Trek III: The Search For Spock and wrote the screenplays for all three motion pictures he directed. His last major work was 1997's Aurora, based on his book Moon.

In the 1950s, after the Cold War ended and America entered its post-industrial free fall, a small family in California set about reviving a once thriving trade route by bringing food, livestock, manufactured goods, and electronics from Mexico through Texas to be shipped to the city's industrial center, Monterrey. But when their business begins falling apart — suddenly losing their customers and suffering major accidents — the company grows distant over time until they leave as a broken family. While searching for answers to explain their current circumstances, relatives discover some startling details. They discover that this new generation has always known how valuable and irreplaceable Mexican produce, especially fruit and vegetables, were to this country, but no one knew anything about how much it cost or where exactly in Texas supplies could find storage space. And not only does the supply chain include Mexico, but there are also hidden caches of valuable supplies scattered throughout northern Baja California Sur and northern New Mexico. With an understanding of what this information means, the family must make some tough choices with their family, friends, fellow families, government officials, and all other possible parties as they try to ensure the continued availability of cheap Mexican food in America's booming urban centres. ~Written by Peter Wacks Advertisements

Plot synopsis for Settling Accounts, by David J. Peterson A man comes ashore on New Zealand Island and, in the process, becomes involved with the people he befriends before being killed during the war that followed his arrival and that began a four-year exodus to New Zealand. The reader learns about various tribes as an element of this story. The narrative shifts between these tribes' struggles for survival and attempts at reconciliation, often with the aid of their gods (and/or supernatural forces), in a nonstop barrage of action and adventure scenes. This first person narrator keeps providing new perspectives into both sides. He is never simply a passive observer: even when attempting to remain calm or composed, he can't stay out of sight forever. When it's time to do something risky or dangerous, his voice says exactly what needs saying. At some point, even the reader might start wondering what happened to him so soon; you know when just right now, there will be more chapters detailing how the hero came back from his death. With just a bit more imagination than I found acceptable while writing all this, the hero himself might have popped up, ready with another long chapter telling how the hero avoided the authorities... This may not actually happen, of course... but one could very well wonder whether it will.

Hovering on the edge, the island of Alohegan lays between two great oceans—the ocean to the east and west, in fact. For all practical purposes the sea between them no longer exists; only the water that had flowed together was ever present. This inland lake, which formed part of an ancient continent of unknown origin, has since receded under its own weight into Lake Oa. It's a small body of water and no more than forty kilometers long by four across, but there are no natural boundaries between it and that vast gulf in the West. The only barrier here, besides the wind, is the cold foggy atmosphere around this small island, which causes both vessels traversing the lakes and soldiers crossing the mountains or deserts below them to become lost, to fall into the maw of death where all that once lived has been buried forever. In an earlier century, there were four great powers in Europe - Portugal under Alfonso V (1232-63), Spain under Philip II (1389-74), France under Charles VII of France (1561-65) and Russia under Romanov dynasty's (1721-60). While many considered that most of those lands had been conquered by others, the Portuguese themselves resisted the advance of their rivals until eventually being pushed into the Atlantic Ocean to be taken over by France in 1567. During this period, the French established their own empire, the Spanish followed shortly thereafter, finally falling under the hand of England at around the end of the century and Italy being absorbed into modern day France. The fate of all these nations was left in the hands of a new breed of European monarchs, the Medici family. By 1598, Spain and France were ruled directly through their respective Barons from Florence, while France and later Germany would still use indirect methods such as the Holy Roman Empire with Habsburg monarchy still having some sway over the other northern and western parts of Europe like Poland and Lithuania. The Galactic Senate, which was responsible for overseeing the Senate's powers and functions, dissolved after the Yuuzhan Vong War. The new galactic government consisted of two chambers: [B]ackbencher"s—a legislative body of senators representing all areas of space with a voice in decisions about national defense and taxation; or "consul[T]eam"—a representative group that held both these offices plus additional responsibilities such as foreign affairs. One hundred members served without pay.[1] In the twenty-year-long Clone Wars between the Galactic Republic vs. the Confederacy of Independent Systems (aka Sith Empire), Senator Padmé Amidala became the first elected member of the Grand Military Council and later, Chief of State. Senator Palpatine had long advocated using an electoral system where each senator earned his or her seat on the council based upon support among their constituency and not according to voting patterns. As a result, this policy also proved controversial. Under the leadership of former Supreme Chancellor Finis Valorum the war between the Confederacy and the Galactic Republic became increasingly bloody over several years. With increasing tension during this time the Senate debated the merits of political versus military institutions so it could decide whether to place military command in the hands of one man or have a democratically elected council. Plot Summary [ edit ] Fourteen years after the fall of New York City, former football star Bobby Knight struggles to regain and hold up his NFL Hall of Fame championship ring in San Francisco. One morning, Knight finds that he has been stripped of the title by an organization called the "Tough Guys". It's then revealed that the group was behind the recent attacks on the public housing projects throughout the city, which have led to one hundred thirty three deaths of residents. At the same time, Knight learns that he will inherit his championship in 2033; this news leads him to set out on a mission of revenge and find the man who murdered his family, hoping to end this injustice once and for all by killing the man himself! Set in Eastern Europe during WWII, "Sassinak" revolves around the life and exploits of an SS major named Raul (who is actually a civilian whose family was deported to Nazi concentration camps); his wife Foscha ("Foschian"); an American girl at his base camp nicknamed "Ragtime"; their friend Dvoraya; an American prisoner named Bobo, who helped rescue Foschi's dog Paddy; the young and ambitious sergeant Nikolai Vainov (whom we meet at age 25 because his brother committed suicide while serving as a fighter pilot), whose wartime diary helps Vainov come to terms with what he has experienced; and some of the German officers involved in Vainov's training or service, including a decorated general. The book uses a wide variety of settings—including Romania and New York City—as well as real-life figures such as Adolf Eichmann, Klaus Barbie and Hans-Joachim Lippe, in order to weave the main conflict into familiar narrative events that are seen as more realistic than those typical of many war novels. [Author] Peter Cvetnyak created a vivid atmosphere through his use of evocative images, often drawn directly from public records; and a careful combination of realism and mythic elements ensures that this fictional account comes together convincingly. [End of Plot Summary]

Together they manage a hotel and hide, along with the others to make sure the other passengers on board do not realize that something very bad (much worse than the ship's malfunctioning engines) is happening onboard. Vespers: Vespers is the sixth Sunday of Easter, the longest service in the Church calendar (it usually takes place on Saturday, and is traditionally read in the evening). On this day, in the ancient Greek and Latin church, liturgical services are held. The service begins with a reading of the Gospel from the letter of Saint Luke (according to tradition, Luke wrote this letter upon his departure from Jerusalem). Other readings then include passages from the Old Testament and from the writings of St. Paul. Vespers is followed by the Gospel of John (the first of those books that Paul wrote), followed by the Book of Matthew, and then the Book of Luke. These are followed by the "Gospel of St. John", again the first of those books that Paul wrote. Fasting is a daily practice that is considered particularly important during the celebration of Vespers. During the celebration of Vespers, the faithful abstain from eating foods and drinks during the daytime in order to show submission towards God and be purified in preparation for the rest of their day. This practice is most commonly observed on the first, sixth, and tenth days of the week, but it is also observed on all Sundays. In addition to abstaining from food and drink, the faithful are ordered to pray to God, which can be done before they go to bed and at the beginning of the day or in the evening. […] [T]he Maccabees were led by "Simon" whom Jesus entrusted with power over the Temple complex and "the two goats which are in the temple"—they are mentioned only in connection to Jesus in the gospels and are never identified by him. In fact, a tradition of Simon and his followers who, in the second century, attacked and destroyed a temple dedicated to Apollo in Rome. This temple, which is known to have been built by a priestess of Jupiter, was dedicated to the Greek god Zeus and, in fact, the story in the gospels of Jesus setting fire to, and even "crushing to death" the priestess of Apollo, who was worshipping a stone statue of the god, recalls these events. The second Maccabees is a story of one of Jesus's companions, Simon of Cyrene, whose first act of patriotism would include setting fire to an ancient Jewish shrine to Apollo. A book by British novelist John Le Carré that shows how a series of covert British government interventions (1946-1954) helped to create Pakistan and the state of Afghanistan. When the French occupation ended, Pakistan's political and security situation was very weak; the Indian establishment was still very weak; and it only began to improve in the 1960s as the influence of the United States declined. This book depicts how, in what turned out to be a deliberate and successful British plan, covert operations played an important role in enabling Pakistan to emerge as one of the two major political powers, the other being India, in the early 1960s. (I say "a concerted plan," since a great number of individual acts - which don't usually fit into a single coherent strategy - were part of it.) These operations were planned with the help of British intelligence, and were conducted by MI6, special forces, and in some cases by the special branch of the Pakistani police and the Army. The operation had two elements. First, Pakistan was to be brought back into the fold of the Western World and it was also to acquire military aid for its military independence. The second element of the plan was to create conflict between India and Pakistan (as a pretext for partition) and for the formation of the "Great Game." What happened? "Someone gained unauthorised access to our internal network," said Thomas Verrilli, head of corporate counsel at US telecoms firm USTelecom, adding that this followed "a period of heightened activity" between November 12 and December 6.
